The axioms for right ( n + 𝟐 ) \bm{(n+2)} -angulated categories † † footnotetext: Jing He was supported by the Hunan Provincial Natural Science Foundation of China (Grant No. 2023JJ40217).
Jing He, Jiangsha Li

Abstract
Drawing inspiration from the works of Beligiannis–Marmaridis and Lin, we refine the axioms for a right ( n + 2 ) (n+2) -angulated category and give some examples of such categories. Interestingly, we show that the morphism axiom for a right ( n + 2 ) (n+2) -angulated category is actually redundant. Moreover, we prove that the higher “octahedral axiom” is equivalent to the mapping cone axiom for a right ( n + 2 ) (n+2) -angulated category. Keywords: ( n + 2 ) (n+2) -angulated category; right ( n + 2 ) (n+2) -angulated category; quotient category; morphism axiom; higher octahedral axiom; mapping cone axiom 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: 18G80



1
Introduction

The notion of a triangulated category was introduced in the mid 1960’s by Verdier
in his thesis
[
V
]
. Having their origins in algebraic geometry and algebraic topology, triangulated categories have now become indispensable in many different areas of mathematics.
Assem, Beligiannis and Marmaridis
[
ABM
,
BM
]
defined
the notion of right triangulated category. Informally, a right triangulated category is a triangulated category whose suspension functor is not necessarily an automorphism.
Let
{\rm mod}\Lambda
be the category of finitely generated right

\Lambda
-modules over an artin algebra
\Lambda
.
Beligiannis and Marmaridis
[
BM
]
proved that
any covariantly finite subcategory
\mathscr{X}
of
{\rm mod}\Lambda
induces a right triangulated category
on the quotient category
{\rm mod}\Lambda/\mathscr{X}
.
Later, this result was extended by Beligiannis and Reiten
[
BR
]
to a more general situation, namely, if
\mathcal{A}
is an abelian category and
\mathscr{X}
is contravariantly finite in
\mathcal{A}
,
then the quotient category
\mathcal{A}/\mathscr{X}
is a right triangulated category.

Recently, Geiss, Keller and Oppermann
[
GKO
]
introduced the notion of an
(n+2)
-angulated category, which is a higher dimensional analogue of a triangulated category.
We note that the case
n=1
corresponds to a triangulated category.
A primary source of examples for
(n+2)
-angulated categories is
n
-cluster tilting subcategories of triangulated categories that are closed under the
n
th power of the shift functor.
Building on the concept of a right triangulated category, Lin
[
L2
]
defined a right
(n+2)
-angulated category and explored those that arise from a covariantly finite subcategory.

In this paper, we refine the axioms for a right
(n+2)
-angulated category as defined by Lin.
Moreover, we also provide some examples of a right
(n+2)
-angulated category.
The morphism axiom of an
(n+2)
-angulated category states that a morphism between
the bases of two
(n+2)
-angles can be extended to a morphism of
(n+2)
-angles.
Arentz-Hansen, Bergh and Thaule
[
AHBT
]
showed that the morphism axiom for an
(n+2)
-angulated category is redundant. Based on this idea, we prove that the conclusion holds true in a right
(n+2)
-angulated category.
Bergh and Thaule
[
BT
]
showed that the higher “octahedral axiom” is equivalent to the mapping cone axiom in an
(n+2)
-angulated category. We explain how this generalizes to right
(n+2)
-angulated categories. However, our proof method differs from that used in the case of the
(n+2)
-angulated category. Additionally, we present another equivalent characterization of the mapping cone axiom.

The paper is organized as follows:
In Section 2, we provide an overview of the definitions of the
(n+2)
-angulated category, the
n
-cokernel, and the special
n
-cokernel.
In Section 3, we refine the definition of a right
(n+2)
-angulated category.
In Section 4, we present some examples of right
(n+2)
-angulated categories.
In Section 5, we prove the redundancy of the morphism axiom in a right
(n+2)
-angulated category.
In Section 6, we give some new equivalent statements of the
higher mapping cone axiom.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we recall
the axioms for
(n+2)
-angulated categories as described in
[
GKO
,
BT
]
.
Let
\mathscr{C}
be an additive category with an automorphism
\Sigma:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
, and
n
be an positive integer. A sequence of objects and morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
of the form

A_{0}\xrightarrow{~a_{0}~}A_{1}\xrightarrow{~a_{1}~}A_{2}\xrightarrow{~a_{2}~}\cdots\xrightarrow{~a_{n-1}~}A_{n}\xrightarrow{~a_{n}~}A_{n+1}\xrightarrow{~a_{n+1}~}\Sigma A_{0}

is called an
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-
sequence
. Its
left rotation
is in the form of the following

A_{1}\xrightarrow{~a_{1}~}A_{2}\xrightarrow{~a_{2}~}A_{3}\xrightarrow{~a_{3}~}\cdots\xrightarrow{~a_{n}~}A_{n+1}\xrightarrow{~a_{n+1}~}\Sigma A_{0}\xrightarrow{(-1)^{n}\Sigma a_{0}}\Sigma A_{1}.

A
morphism
of
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequences is a sequence
f=(f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{n+1})
in
\mathscr{C}
such that the following diagram

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma f_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{1}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n-1}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma B_{0}}

commutes. It is an
isomorphism
if
f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{n+1}
are all isomorphisms in
\mathscr{C}
.

Recently, Geiss, Keller, and Oppermann
[
GKO
]
have discussed the axioms for an
(n+2)
-angulated category. In particular, they introduced a higher “octahedral axiom” and showed that it is equivalent to the mapping cone axiom for an
(n+2)
-angulated category, as seen in
[
BT
, Theorem 4.4]
. Let’s now recall the definition of an
(n+2)
-angulated category.

Definition 2.1
.

[
GKO
,
BT
]

An
(n+2)
-
angulated

category
is a triple
(\mathscr{C},\Sigma,\Phi)
, where
\mathscr{C}
is an additive category,
\Sigma
is an automorphism of
\mathscr{C}
, and
\Phi
is a class of
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequences (whose elements are called
(n+2)
-
angles
), which satisfies the following axioms:

(N1)

(a)

The class
\Phi
is closed under isomorphisms, direct sums and direct summands.

(b)

For any object
A\in\mathscr{C}
, the following trivial sequence

A\xrightarrow{1}A\rightarrow 0\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\Sigma A

belongs to
\Phi
.

(c)

Each morphism
a_{0}\colon A_{0}\rightarrow A_{1}
in
\mathscr{C}
, there exists an
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequence in

\Phi
whose first morphism is
a_{0}
.

(N2)

An
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequence belongs to
\Phi
if and only if its left rotation belongs to
\Phi
.

(N3)

Given the solid part of the commutative diagram

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma f_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{1}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n-1}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma B_{0}}

with rows in
\Phi
. Then there exist the dotted morphisms such that the above diagram commutes i.e.
(f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{n+1})
is a morphism of
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequences.

(N4)

Given a commutative diagram

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma f_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{c_{1}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n-1}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}}
\textstyle{C_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{2}}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{n-1}}
\textstyle{C_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{n}}
\textstyle{C_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{1}}

with the first two rows and the second column in
\Phi
. Then there exist morphisms

f_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow B_{i}~~(i=2,3,\cdots,n+1)

\displaystyle g_{i}:B_{i}\rightarrow C_{i}~~(i=2,3,\cdots,n+1)

\displaystyle h_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow C_{i-1}~~(i=3,4,\cdots,n+1)

with the following two properties:

(a)

The sequence
(f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{n+1})
is a morphism of
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequences;

(b)

The following
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequence

A_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{3}\oplus B_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}\\
h_{3}&g_{2}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{4}\oplus B_{3}\oplus C_{2}\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{1}~}A_{5}\oplus B_{4}\oplus C_{3}\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{2}~}\cdots\hskip 56.9055pt

\cdots\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{n-3}~}A_{n+1}\oplus B_{n}\oplus C_{n-1}\xrightarrow{~\beta~}B_{n+1}\oplus C_{n}\xrightarrow{\left[g_{n+1}~c_{n}\right]}C_{n+1}\xrightarrow{\Sigma a_{1}\circ c_{n+1}}\Sigma A_{2}

belongs to
\Phi
, where

\alpha_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{i+3}&0&0\\[2.84526pt]
(-1)^{i}f_{i+3}&-b_{i+2}&0\\[2.84526pt]
h_{i+3}&g_{i+2}&c_{i+1}\\
\end{matrix}\right],~\beta=\left[\begin{matrix}(-1)^{n}f_{n+1}&-b_{n}&0\\[2.84526pt]
h_{n+1}&g_{n}&c_{n-1}\\
\end{matrix}\right],

and
c_{n+1}g_{n+1}=\Sigma a_{0}\circ b_{n+1}
.

Arentz-Hansen, Bergh and Thaule mentioned the following in their paper
[
AHBT
]
: “The following axiom (N4
∗
) is not strictly the same as axiom (N4) in
[
BT
]
. However, it follows from the proofs in
[
BT
, Section 4]
that the two are equivalent.”
Unfortunately, there is no detailed proof provided, so for the convenience of the readers, we present a detailed proof.

(N4
∗
)

Given the solid part of the following commutative diagram

(\spadesuit)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma A_{1}}
a_{0}
a_{1}
f_{1}
a_{2}
f_{2}
a_{3}
f_{3}
a_{n-1}
a_{n}
f_{n}
a_{n+1}
f_{n+1}
b_{0}
a_{0}
b_{1}
b_{2}
g_{2}
b_{3}
g_{3}
b_{n-1}
b_{n}
g_{n}
b_{n+1}
g_{n+1}
\Sigma a_{0}
f_{1}
c_{1}
c_{2}
c_{3}
c_{n-1}
c_{n}
c_{n+1}
h_{3}
h_{n+1}

with rows in
\Phi
, there exist the dotted
morphisms such that each square commutes, and the following

(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequence

A_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{3}\oplus B_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}\\
h_{3}&g_{2}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{4}\oplus B_{3}\oplus C_{2}\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{1}~}A_{5}\oplus B_{4}\oplus C_{3}\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{2}~}\cdots\hskip 56.9055pt

\cdots\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{n-3}~}A_{n+1}\oplus B_{n}\oplus C_{n-1}\xrightarrow{~\beta~}B_{n+1}\oplus C_{n}\xrightarrow{\left[g_{n+1}~c_{n}\right]}C_{n+1}\xrightarrow{\Sigma a_{1}\circ c_{n+1}}\Sigma A_{2}

belongs to
\Phi
, where

\alpha_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{i+3}&0&0\\[2.84526pt]
(-1)^{i}f_{i+3}&-b_{i+2}&0\\[2.84526pt]
h_{i+3}&g_{i+2}&c_{i+1}\\
\end{matrix}\right],~\beta=\left[\begin{matrix}(-1)^{n}f_{n+1}&-b_{n}&0\\[2.84526pt]
h_{n+1}&g_{n}&c_{n-1}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
.

The following result show that axiom (N4) may be replaced by axiom (N4
∗
).

Theorem 2.2
.

If
\Phi
is a collection of
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequences satisfying axioms
(N1), (N2)
and
(N3)
, then
\Phi
satisfies
(N4)
if and only if

\Phi
satisfies
(N4
∗
)
.

Proof.

Sufficiency.
This is evident.

Necessity
. By (N4
∗
), we have the following
commutative diagram

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern (2.2)

where rows in
\Phi
, by
[
BT
, Lemma 4.1]
, there exist morphisms
g_{i}:B_{i}\rightarrow C_{i}~~(i=2,\cdots,n+1)
, and
h_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow C_{i-1}~~(i=3,\cdots,n+1)
make the diagram (
2.2
) commutes, and the following mapping cone

A_{2}\oplus B_{1}\oplus A_{1}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{2}&0&0\\
-f_{2}&-b_{1}&0\\
0&1&f_{1}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{3}\oplus B_{2}\oplus B_{1}\xrightarrow{~~\alpha_{0}~~}A_{4}\oplus B_{3}\oplus C_{2}\xrightarrow{~~\alpha_{1}~~}\cdots\hskip 142.26378pt

\cdots\xrightarrow{\alpha_{n-3}}A_{n+1}\oplus B_{n}\oplus C_{n-1}\xrightarrow{\beta}B_{n+1}\oplus C_{n}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-\Sigma a_{0}b_{n+1}&0\\
g_{n+1}&c_{n}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\Sigma A_{1}\oplus C_{n+1}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\Sigma a_{1}&0\\
-\Sigma f_{1}&0\\
1&c_{n+1}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\Sigma A_{2}\oplus\Sigma B_{1}\oplus\Sigma A_{1}

belongs to
\Phi
, where
\alpha_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{i+3}&0&0\\[2.84526pt]

\end{matrix}\right],~\beta=\left[\begin{matrix}(-1)^{n}f_{n+1}&-b_{n}&0\\[2.84526pt]
h_{n+1}&g_{n}&c_{n-1}\\
\end{matrix}\right].

Since the diagram (
2.2
) commutes, we have the following
equalities.

\displaystyle\left[\begin{matrix}h_{3}&g_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}a_{2}&0\\
f_{2}&b_{1}\\
\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}h_{3}a_{2}+g_{2}f_{2}&g_{2}b_{1}\\
\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}0&c_{1}\\
\end{matrix}\right]~~\Rightarrow~~g_{2}b_{1}=c_{1};

\displaystyle\left[\begin{matrix}h_{4}&g_{3}\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}a_{3}&0\\
-f_{3}&b_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}h_{4}a_{3}+g_{3}f_{3}&g_{3}b_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}c_{2}h_{3}&c_{2}g_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]~~\Rightarrow~~g_{3}b_{2}=c_{2}g_{2};

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\left[\begin{matrix}(-1)^{n+1}g_{n+1}f_{n+1}&g_{n+1}b_{n}\\
\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}c_{n}h_{n+1}&c_{n}g_{n}\\
\end{matrix}\right]~~\Rightarrow~~g_{n+1}b_{n}=c_{n}g_{n};

\displaystyle c_{n+1}g_{n+1}=\Sigma a_{0}\circ b_{n+1}.

Thus we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Remark 2.3
.

In (N4), it’s hard to see that the square
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\spadesuit)
is commutative. With
\Sigma
being an automorphism, it ensures that
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\spadesuit)
is commutative; otherwise, it cannot be obtained.

3
Right
(n+2)
-angulated categories

Based on Theorem
2.2
, we refine the definition of a right
(n+2)
-angulated category, introducing some axioms that differ from the original definition
[
L2
, Definition 2.1]
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
right
(n+2)
-angulated category
is a triple
(\mathscr{C},\Sigma,\Theta)
, where
\mathscr{C}
is an additive category,
\Sigma
is an endofunctor of
\mathscr{C}
, and
\Theta
is a class of
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequences (whose elements are called
right
(n+2)
-angles
), which satisfies the following axioms:

(RN1)

(a)

The class
\Theta
is closed under isomorphisms, direct sums and direct summands.

(b
∗
)

For any object
A\in\mathscr{C}
, the following trivial sequence

0\rightarrow A\xrightarrow{1}A\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow 0

belongs to
\Theta
.

(c)

Each morphism
a_{0}:A_{0}\rightarrow A_{1}
in
\mathscr{C}
can be embedded in a right
(n+2)
-angle, and
a_{0}
is the first morphism.

(RN2)

If an
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequence belongs to
\Theta
, then its left rotation belongs to
\Theta
.

(RN3)

(morphism axiom)
Given the solid part of the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma f_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{1}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n-1}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n}}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma B_{0}}

with rows in
\Theta
. Then there exist the dotted morphisms such that the above diagram commutes. i.e.
(f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{n+1})
is a morphism of
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequences.

(RN4
∗
)

(octahedral axiom)
Given the solid part of the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma A_{1}}
a_{0}

b_{n-1}
b_{n}
g_{n}
b_{n+1}
g_{n+1}
\Sigma a_{0}
f_{1}
c_{1}
c_{2}
c_{3}
c_{n-1}

with commuting squares and rows in
\Theta
, then there exist the dotted morphisms such that each square commutes, and the following

(n+2)

-sequence

A_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}a_{2}\\

\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{4}\oplus B_{3}\oplus C_{2}\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{1}~}A_{5}\oplus B_{4}\oplus C_{3}\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{2}~}\cdots\hskip 56.9055pt

\cdots\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{n-3}~}A_{n+1}\oplus B_{n}\oplus C_{n-1}\xrightarrow{~\beta~}B_{n+1}\oplus C_{n}\xrightarrow{\left[g_{n+1}~c_{n}\right]}C_{n+1}\xrightarrow{\Sigma a_{1}\circ c_{n+1}}\Sigma A_{2}

belongs to
\Theta
, where

\alpha_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{i+3}&0&0\\[2.84526pt]
(-1)^{i}f_{i+3}&-b_{i+2}&0\\[2.84526pt]
h_{i+3}&g_{i+2}&c_{i+1}\\
\end{matrix}\right],~\beta=\left[\begin{matrix}(-1)^{n}f_{n+1}&-b_{n}&0\\[2.84526pt]
h_{n+1}&g_{n}&c_{n-1}\\
\end{matrix}\right].

Remark 3.2
.

Comparing
[
L2
, Definition 2.1]
, the conditions (RN1)(b
∗
) and (RN4
∗
) are different from those in
[
L2
]
. Now we state those two conditions as presented in their paper. In
[
L2
]
, (RN1)(b) takes the following form:

A\xrightarrow{1}A\rightarrow 0\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\Sigma A

and (RN4) is represented as: Given a commutative diagram

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{c_{1}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n-1}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}}
\textstyle{C_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{2}}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{n-1}}
\textstyle{C_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{n}}
\textstyle{C_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{1}}

with the first two rows and the second column in
\Theta
. Then there exist morphisms

\displaystyle f_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow B_{i}~~(i=2,3,\cdots,n+1)

\displaystyle g_{i}:B_{i}\rightarrow C_{i}~~(i=2,3,\cdots,n+1)

\displaystyle h_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow C_{i-1}~~(i=3,4,\cdots,n+1)

with the following two properties:

(a)

The sequence
(f_{0},f_{1},\cdots,f_{n+1})
is a morphism of
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequences;

(b)

The following
(n+2)
-
\Sigma
-sequence

A_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{3}\oplus B_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}\\
h_{3}&g_{2}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{4}\oplus B_{3}\oplus C_{2}\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{1}~}A_{5}\oplus B_{4}\oplus C_{3}\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{2}~}\cdots\hskip 56.9055pt

\cdots\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{n-3}~}A_{n+1}\oplus B_{n}\oplus C_{n-1}\xrightarrow{~\beta~}B_{n+1}\oplus C_{n}\xrightarrow{\left[g_{n+1}~c_{n}\right]}C_{n+1}\xrightarrow{\Sigma a_{1}\circ c_{n+1}}\Sigma A_{2}

belongs to
\Theta
,
where

\alpha_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{i+3}&0&0\\[2.84526pt]
(-1)^{i}f_{i+3}&-b_{i+2}&0\\[2.84526pt]
h_{i+3}&g_{i+2}&c_{i+1}\\
\end{matrix}\right],~\beta=\left[\begin{matrix}(-1)^{n}f_{n+1}&-b_{n}&0\\[2.84526pt]
h_{n+1}&g_{n}&c_{n-1}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
,

and
c_{n+1}g_{n+1}=\Sigma a_{0}\circ b_{n+1}
.

Remark 3.3
.

(1) In
[
BM
]
, the right triangulated category defines a trivial sequence of any object
A
in
\mathscr{C}
as follows:

0\rightarrow A\xrightarrow{1}A\rightarrow 0

Therefore, we define the trivial sequence in a right
(n+2)
-angulated category in the form given in (RN1)(b
∗
), and when (RN1)(b
∗
) holds, so does (RN1)(b).

(2) In Theorem
2.2
,
we know that if
\Phi
satisfies (N1), (N2) and (N3), then (N4) and (N4
∗
) are equivalent. So we adopt (N4
∗
) to define the right
(n+2)
-angulated category.
Note that (RN4) and (RN4
∗
) are not identical. It is easy to observe that (RN4
∗
) implies (RN4) is straightforward.
Since
\Sigma
is not an automorphism, it is challenging to ensure the reverse direction holds, making our definition stricter than the original one. Additionally, when
n=1
, our definition is in perfect alignment with the right triangulated category in the sense of
Beligiannis and Marmaridis
[
BM
, Definition 1.1]
.

(3) If
\Sigma
is an automorphism, and the condition (RN2) also holds the opposite.
In this case, the right
(n+2)
-angulated category is an
(n+2)
-angulated category in the sense of
[
GKO
,
AHBT
]
.

4
Right
(n+2)
-angulated quotient categories

In this section, we give some examples of
(n+2)
-angulated categories.
We need some preparations as follows.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an additive category and
f:A\rightarrow B
is a morphism in
\mathscr{C}
.
Recall that
g:B\rightarrow C
is a
weak cokernel
of
f
, if
gf=0
and for each morphism
h:B\rightarrow X
such that
hf=0
there exists a morphism
s:C\rightarrow X
(not necessarily unique) such that
sg=h
. These are reflected in the commutative diagram below

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 11.38109ptf\hskip 8.19447pt\;\;}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g\;\;}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{X}

Note that
g
is a
cokernel
of
f
if
g
is an epimorphism. We can define
weak kernel
dually.

.

Lemma 4.3
.

[
L2
, Lemma 2.8]

Let

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{n+1}}
\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{1}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n-1}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}}

be a commutative diagram of right
n
-exact sequences. Then we have an
n
-pushout diagram as follows

\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{n+1}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{1}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n-1}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}.}

That is to say,

A_{1}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{1}\\
f_{1}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{2}\oplus B_{1}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{2}&0\\
f_{2}&b_{1}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{3}\oplus B_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&b_{2}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\cdots\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{n}&0\\
f_{n}&b_{n-1}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{n+1}\oplus B_{n}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}f_{n+1}&~b_{n}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}B_{n+1}

is a right
n
-exact sequence.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an additive category and
\mathscr{X}
be a subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
. In this paper, when we say
\mathscr{X}
is a subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
, it usually means that
\mathscr{X}
is full, and is closed under isomorphisms, direct sums and direct summands.
In the quotient category
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}
, the objects are the same as those in
\mathscr{C}
, and the morphisms are elements in
\mathscr{C}(A,B)/\mathscr{X}(A,B)
, where
\mathscr{X}(A,B)
forms a subgroup of
\mathscr{C}(A,B)
, and these morphisms pass through objects within
\mathscr{X}
. We denote
\overline{f}
as the image of the morphism
f:A\rightarrow B
under the quotient functor
\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}
.

A morphism
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathscr{C}
is called
\mathscr{X}
-
monic
if the sequence
\mathscr{C}(B,X)\xrightarrow{\mathscr{C}(f,X)}\mathscr{C}(A,X)\rightarrow 0
is exact for any object
X
in
\mathscr{X}
. Additionally, the morphism
f
is referred to as a
left

\mathscr{X}
-
approximation
of
A
if
B
belongs to
\mathscr{X}
.
The subcategory
\mathscr{X}
is called
covariantly finite
of
\mathscr{C}
, if any object
A\in\mathscr{C}
has a left
\mathscr{X}
-approximation. We can define
\mathscr{X}
-epic, right
\mathscr{X}
-approximation and cintravariantly finite
dually.

Definition 4.4
.

[
L2
, Proposition 3.1]

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an additive category, and
\mathscr{X}
be a covariantly finite subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
. If every left
\mathscr{X}
-approximation has a special
n
-cokernel with respect to
\mathscr{X}
, then there exist an additive endofunctor
\Sigma:\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}
, defined as follows:

For any object
A\in\mathscr{C}
, there exist a right
n
-exact sequence

A\xrightarrow{a_{0}}X_{1}\xrightarrow{a_{1}}X_{2}\xrightarrow{a_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{a_{n-1}}X_{n}\xrightarrow{a_{n}}B

where
a_{0}
is a left
\mathscr{X}
-approximation of
A
and
(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{n})
is a special
n
-cokernel of
a_{0}
.

For any morphism
f\colon A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
, since
a_{0}
is a left
\mathscr{X}
-approximation of
A
, we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{X_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}.}

Define an endofunctor
\Sigma\colon\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}
such that
\Sigma A=B
and
\Sigma\overline{f}=\overline{g}
.

Definition 4.5
.

[
L2
, Definition 3.2]

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an additive category, and
\mathscr{X}
be a covariantly finite subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
. Assume that every left
\mathscr{X}
-approximation has a special
n
-cokernel with respect to
\mathscr{X}
.
Let

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}}

is a right
n
-exact sequence, where
a_{0}
is
\mathscr{X}
-monic. Then there exist the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{n+1}}
\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n-1}}
\textstyle{X_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}}

where
b_{0}
is a left
\mathscr{X}
-approximation of
A
,
(b_{1},b_{2},\cdots,b_{n})
is a special
n
-cokernel of
b_{0}
. Then we have a complex

A_{0}\xrightarrow{~\overline{a_{0}}~}A_{1}\xrightarrow{~\overline{a_{1}}~}A_{2}\xrightarrow{~\overline{a_{2}}~}\cdots\xrightarrow{~\overline{a_{n}}~}A_{n+1}\xrightarrow{(-1)^{n}\overline{f_{n+1}}}\Sigma A_{0}.

We define
right
(n+2)
-angles
in
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}
as the complexes which are isomorphic to
complexes obtained in this way. We denote by
\Theta
the class of right
(n+2)
-angles.

Lemma 4.6
.

[
L2
, Lemma 3.3]

Let

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{n+1}}
\textstyle{B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{1}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n-1}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}}

be a commutative diagram of right
n
-exact sequences, where
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
are
\mathscr{X}
-monic. Then we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{0}}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{1}}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{2}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{n-1}}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{n}}\hskip 5.69054pt}

\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{n+1}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\overline{f_{0}}}
\textstyle{B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{0}}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{1}}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{2}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{n-1}}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{n}}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n}\overline{b_{n+1}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma B_{0}}

of right
(n+2)
-angles in
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}
.

The following result can be found in
[
L2
, Theorem 3.4]
.
Now we refine this result to satisfy our definition of a right
(n+2)
-angulated category
as stated in Definition
3.1
.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an additive category and
\mathscr{X}
be a covariantly finite subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
. If every
\mathscr{X}
-monic morphism has an
n
-cokernel and every left
\mathscr{X}
-approximation has a special
n
-cokernel with respect to
\mathscr{X}
, then the quotient category
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}
is a right
(n+2)
-angulated category with respect to the endofunctor
\Sigma
defined in Definition
4.4
and right
(n+2)
-angles defined in Definition
4.5
.

Proof.

We will now check the axioms of right
(n+2)
-angulated categories
as defined in Definition
3.1
.
We only need to prove (RN1)(b
∗
) and (RN4
∗
), as the proofs for the other axioms are the same as the ones provided in
[
L2
, Theorem 3.4]
and can be omitted.

The commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

shows that

0\rightarrow A\xrightarrow{1}A\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow 0

belongs to
\Theta
. Thus (RN1)(b
∗
) holds.

Now we prove that (RN4
∗
) holds. Given the solid part of the diagram

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{0}}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{1}}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{2}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{n}}}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n}\overline{a_{n+1}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{1}a_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{0}}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{1}}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{2}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{n}}}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n}\overline{b_{n+1}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\overline{a_{0}}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{1}}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{c_{1}}}
\textstyle{C_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{c_{2}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{c_{n}}}
\textstyle{C_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n}\overline{c_{n+1}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{1}}

with commuting squares and with rows in

.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that

and

are
\mathscr{X}
-monic.
Thus we obtain that

(4.2)

with rows are right
n
-exact sequences. By the property of weak cokernels, there exist
g_{i}:B_{i}\rightarrow C_{i}~~(i=2,\cdots,n+1),h_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow C_{i-1}~~(i=3,\cdots,n+1)
such that the diagram (
4.2
) commutes.
Since the diagram (
4.2
) is commutative,
we have the follow equalities:

\displaystyle\left[\begin{matrix}h_{3}&g_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}a_{2}&0\\
f_{2}&b_{1}\\
\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}h_{3}a_{2}+g_{2}f_{2}&g_{2}b_{1}\\
\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}0&c_{1}\\
\end{matrix}\right]~~\Rightarrow~~g_{2}b_{1}=c_{1};

\displaystyle\left[\begin{matrix}h_{4}&g_{3}\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}a_{3}&0\\
-f_{3}&b_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}h_{4}a_{3}-g_{3}f_{3}&g_{3}b_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}c_{2}h_{3}&c_{2}g_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]~~\Rightarrow~~g_{3}b_{2}=c_{2}g_{2};

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\left[\begin{matrix}(-1)^{n+1}g_{n+1}f_{n+1}&g_{n+1}b_{n}\\
\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}c_{n}h_{n+1}&c_{n}g_{n}\\
\end{matrix}\right]~~\Rightarrow~~g_{n+1}b_{n}=c_{n}g_{n}.

Thus we obtain the following commutative diagram

for the diagram (

\begin{split}\hskip 56.9055pt\cdots\xrightarrow{~\alpha_{n-3}~}A_{n+1}\oplus B_{n}\oplus C_{n-1}\xrightarrow{~\beta~}B_{n+1}\oplus C_{n}\xrightarrow{\left[g_{n+1}~c_{n}\right]}C_{n+1}\end{split}

(4.3)

where

(-1)^{i}f_{i+3}&-b_{i+2}&0\\[2.84526pt]
h_{i+3}&g_{i+2}&c_{i+1}\\
\end{matrix}\right],~~\beta=\left[\begin{matrix}(-1)^{n}f_{n+1}&-b_{n}&0\\[2.84526pt]
h_{n+1}&g_{n}&c_{n-1}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
.

Next we want to show that the following sequence is a right
n
-exact sequence

A_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{3}\oplus B_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}\\
h_{3}&g_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{4}\oplus B_{3}\oplus C_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{4}&0&0\\
-f_{4}&-b_{3}&0\\
h_{4}&g_{3}&c_{2}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\cdots\hskip 113.81102pt

(4.4)

\cdots\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{n}&0&0\\
(-1)^{n-1}f_{n}&-b_{n-1}&0\\
h_{n}&g_{n-1}&c_{n-2}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{n+1}\oplus B_{n}\oplus C_{n-1}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}(-1)^{n}f_{n+1}&-b_{n}&0\\
h_{n+1}&g_{n}&c_{n-1}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}B_{n+1}\oplus C_{n}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}g_{n+1}&c_{n}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}C_{n+1}.

It suffices to prove that
\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}\\
h_{3}&g_{2}\end{matrix}\right]
is a weak cokernel of
\left[\begin{matrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
and
\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{4}&0&0\\
-f_{4}&-b_{3}&0\\
h_{4}&g_{3}&c_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
is a weak cokernel of
\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}\\
h_{3}&g_{2}\end{matrix}\right]
.

Let
\left[\begin{matrix}s&t\\
\end{matrix}\right]:A_{3}\oplus B_{2}\rightarrow M
such that
\left[\begin{matrix}s&t\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]=0
, i.e.
sa_{2}+tf_{2}=0
.
Then we have
\left[\begin{matrix}s&t&tb_{1}\\
\end{matrix}\right]:A_{3}\oplus B_{2}\oplus B_{1}\rightarrow M
such that
\left[\begin{matrix}s&t&tb_{1}\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{2}&0\\
-f_{2}&-b_{1}\\
0&1\\
\end{matrix}\right]=0
, we know that the sequence (
4.3
) is a right
n
-exact sequence, there exist a morphism
\left[\begin{matrix}p&q&r\\
\end{matrix}\right]:A_{4}\oplus B_{3}\oplus C_{2}\rightarrow M
such that

\left[\begin{matrix}p&q&r\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{3}&0&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}&0\\
h_{3}&g_{2}&c_{1}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}s&t&tb_{1}\\
\end{matrix}\right].

It follows that
-pa_{3}+qf_{3}+rh_{3}=s,-qb_{2}+rg_{2}=t,rc_{1}=tb_{1}
.
Then we have

\left[\begin{matrix}p&q&r\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}\\
h_{3}&g_{2}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}s&t\\
\end{matrix}\right].

This is reflected in the commutative diagram below

\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]\hskip 8.19447pt\;\;}
\textstyle{A_{3}\oplus B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}\\
h_{3}&g_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\;\;\hskip 8.53581pt}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}s&t\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{A_{4}\oplus B_{3}\oplus C_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 14.22636pt\left[\begin{smallmatrix}p&q&r\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{M}

This shows that
\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}\\
h_{3}&g_{2}\end{matrix}\right]
is a weak cokernel of
\left[\begin{matrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
.

Let
\left[\begin{matrix}u&v&w\\
\end{matrix}\right]:A_{4}\oplus B_{3}\oplus C_{2}\rightarrow X
such that
\left[\begin{matrix}u&v&w\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}\\
h_{3}&g_{2}\end{matrix}\right]=0
, i.e.
-ua_{3}+vf_{3}+wh_{3}=0,-vb_{2}+wg_{2}=0
.
Then
-vb_{2}b_{1}+wg_{2}b_{1}=0
, i.e.
wc_{1}=0
. Thus we have

\left[\begin{matrix}u&v&w\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{3}&0&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}&0\\
h_{3}&g_{2}&c_{1}\end{matrix}\right]=0,

we know that the sequence (
4.3
) is a right
n
-exact sequence, there exist a morphism
\left[\begin{matrix}l&m&n\\
\end{matrix}\right]:A_{5}\oplus B_{4}\oplus C_{3}\rightarrow X
such that
\left[\begin{matrix}l&m&n\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{4}&0&0\\
-f_{4}&-b_{3}&0\\
h_{4}&g_{3}&c_{2}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}u&v&w\\
\end{matrix}\right]
i.e. we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{A_{3}\oplus B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}\\
h_{3}&g_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\hskip 8.19447pt\;\;}
\textstyle{A_{4}\oplus B_{3}\oplus C_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-a_{4}&0&0\\
-f_{4}&-b_{3}&0\\
h_{4}&g_{3}&c_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\;\;}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}u&v&w\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{A_{4}\oplus B_{3}\oplus C_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 14.22636pt\left[\begin{smallmatrix}l&m&n\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{X}

This shows that
\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{4}&0&0\\
-f_{4}&-b_{3}&0\\
h_{4}&g_{3}&c_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
is a weak cokernel of
\left[\begin{matrix}-a_{3}&0\\
f_{3}&-b_{2}\\
h_{3}&g_{2}\end{matrix}\right]
.
Thus we prove that the sequence (
4.4
) is a right
n
-exact sequence.

We claim that the morphism
\left[\begin{matrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]:A_{2}\rightarrow A_{3}\oplus B_{2}

is
\mathscr{X}
-monic. In fact, for each morphism
s:A_{2}\rightarrow X
, where
X\in\mathscr{X}
, then
sa_{1}:A_{1}\rightarrow X
, since
f_{1}
is
\mathscr{X}
-monic, there exist a morphism
t:B_{1}\rightarrow X
such that
sa_{1}=tf_{1}
. It follows that
tf_{1}a_{0}=sa_{1}a_{0}=0
. So there exist a morphism
r:B_{2}\rightarrow X
such that
rb_{1}=t
. Thus we have

(s-rf_{2})a_{1}=sa_{1}-rf_{2}a_{1}=tf_{1}-rb_{1}f_{1}=tf_{1}-tf_{1}=0.

Then there exist a morphism
k:A_{3}\rightarrow X
such that
ka_{2}=s-rf_{2}
.
It follows that
s=ka_{2}+rf_{2}=\left[\begin{matrix}k&r\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
.
This shows that

\left[\begin{matrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]:A_{2}\rightarrow A_{3}\oplus B_{2}

is
\mathscr{X}
-monic. Hence the right
n
-exact sequence (
4.4
) induces a right
(n+2)
-angle

A_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{a_{2}}\\[2.84526pt]
\overline{f_{2}}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{3}\oplus B_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-\overline{a_{3}}&0\\[2.84526pt]
\overline{f_{3}}&-\overline{b_{2}}\\[2.84526pt]
\overline{h_{3}}&\overline{g_{2}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]}A_{4}\oplus B_{3}\oplus C_{2}\xrightarrow{~\theta_{1}~}\cdots\hskip 142.26378pt

\cdots\xrightarrow{~\theta_{n-3}~}A_{n+1}\oplus B_{n}\oplus C_{n-1}\xrightarrow{~\gamma~}B_{n+1}\oplus C_{n}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{g_{n+1}}~&\overline{c_{n}}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}C_{n+1}\xrightarrow{(-1)^{n}\overline{d_{n+1}}}\Sigma A_{2},

where
\theta_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}-\overline{a_{i+3}}&0&0\\[2.84526pt]
(-1)^{i}\overline{f_{i+3}}&-\overline{b_{i+2}}&0\\[2.84526pt]
\overline{h_{i+3}}&\overline{g_{i+2}}&\overline{c_{i+1}}\\
\end{matrix}\right],~\gamma=\left[\begin{matrix}(-1)^{n}\overline{f_{n+1}}&-\overline{b_{n}}&0\\[2.84526pt]
\overline{h_{n+1}}&\overline{g_{n}}&\overline{c_{n-1}}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
.

For the following commutative diagram of right
n
-exact sequences

\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}

\scriptstyle{c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\

\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{C_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\

Thus we have
\overline{d_{n+1}}=\Sigma\overline{a_{1}}\circ\overline{c_{n+1}}
.
This shows that (RN4

) holds. ∎

In order to give an application, let’s review some concepts from
[
J
]
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
n
-abelian category. An object
I\in\mathcal{A}
is called
injective
if for any monomorphism
f:A\rightarrowtail B
, the sequence

\mathcal{A}(B,I)\xrightarrow{\mathcal{A}(f,\hskip 2.84526ptI)}\mathcal{A}(A,I)\rightarrow 0

is exact. We denote by
\mathcal{I}
the subcategory of injective objects. We say that
\mathcal{A}

has enough injectives
if for any object
A\in\mathcal{A}
, there exists an
n
-exact sequence

A\rightarrowtail I_{1}\rightarrow I_{2}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow I_{n}\twoheadrightarrow B

where
I_{1},I_{2},\cdots,I_{n}\in\mathcal{I}
.

As an application of Theorem
4.7
, we have the following conclusion.

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
n
-abelian category with enough injectives and

\mathcal{I}
be the subcategory of injective objects. Then
the quotient category
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{I}
is a right
(n+2)
-angulated category.

Proof.

Since any morphism in
\mathcal{A}
has
n
-cokernels and every left
\mathcal{I}
-approximation has a special
n
-cokernel with respect to
\mathcal{I}
,
this follows from Theorem
4.7
. ∎

Now let’s review the definition of a strongly covariantly finite
subcategory from
[
LZ
]
.

Definition 4.9
.

[
LZ
, Definition 3.1]

Let
(\mathscr{C},\Sigma,\Phi)
be an
(n+2)
-angulated category. A subcategory
\mathscr{X}
of
\mathscr{C}
is called

strongly covariantly finite
, if for any object
B\in\mathscr{C}
, there exist an
(n+2)
-angle

B\xrightarrow{~f~}X_{1}\xrightarrow{}X_{2}\xrightarrow{}\cdots\xrightarrow{}X_{n}\xrightarrow{~}C\xrightarrow{~}\Sigma B

where
f
is a left
\mathscr{X}
-approximation of
B
and
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{n}\in\mathscr{X}
.

Now we give some examples of strongly covariance finite
subcategories.

Example 4.10
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\Sigma,\Phi)
be an
(n+2)
-angulated category and
\mathscr{X}
be a cluster tilting subcategory (in the sense of Zhou and Zhu
[
ZZ
, Definition 1.1]
) of
\mathscr{C}
.
By Definition
4.9
, we know that
\mathscr{X}
is strongly covariantly finite of
\mathscr{C}
.

Example 4.11
.

This example comes from
[
L1
]
.
Let

\mathcal{T}=D^{b}(kQ)/\tau^{-1}[1]

be the cluster category of type
A_{3}
,
where
Q
is the quiver
1\xrightarrow{~\alpha~}2\xrightarrow{~\beta~}3
,

D^{b}(kQ)
is the bounded derived category of finite generated modules over
kQ
,
\tau
is the Auslander-Reiten translation and
[1]
is the shift functor
of
D^{b}(kQ)
. Then
\mathcal{T}
is a triangulated category. Its shift functor is also denoted by
[1]
.
We describe the
Auslander-Reiten quiver of
\mathcal{T}
in the following:

\textstyle{P_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{3}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{2}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{1}[1]}

It is straightforward to verify that
\mathscr{C}:=\mbox{add}(S_{3}\oplus P_{1}\oplus S_{1})
is a
2
-cluster tilting subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
and satisfies
\mathscr{C}[2]=\mathscr{C}
. By
[
GKO
, Theorem 1]
, we know that
\mathscr{C}
is a
4
-angulated category with an automorphism functor
[2]
.
Let
\mathscr{X}=\mbox{add}(S_{3}\oplus S_{1})
.
Then the
4
-angle

P_{1}\xrightarrow{~~}S_{1}\xrightarrow{~~}S_{3}\xrightarrow{~~}P_{1}\xrightarrow{~~}P_{1}[2]

shows that
\mathscr{X}
is a strongly covariantly finite subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
.

Now we provide another example of a right
(n+2)
-angulated category.

Definition 4.12
.

[
L1
, Proposition 3.3]

Let
(\mathscr{C},\Sigma,\Phi)
be an
(n+2)
-angulated category and
\mathscr{X}
be a strongly covariantly finite subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
. Then there exist an additive endofunctor
\mathbb{G}\colon\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}
, defined as follows:

For any object
A\in\mathscr{C}
, there exists an
(n+2)
-angle

A\xrightarrow{~a_{0}~}X_{1}\xrightarrow{~a_{1}~}X_{2}\xrightarrow{~a_{2}~}\cdots\xrightarrow{~a_{n-1}~}X_{n}\xrightarrow{~a_{n}~}B\xrightarrow{~a_{n+1}~}\Sigma A

where

,

is a left
\mathscr{X}
-approximation of

. For any morphism

, since

is a left

-approximation of

, we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{X_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{a_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma f}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n+1}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A^{\prime}.}

Define an endofunctor
\mathbb{G}\colon\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}
such that
\mathbb{G}A=B
and
\mathbb{G}\overline{f}=\overline{g}
.

Definition 4.13

[

\scriptstyle{a_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n-1}}
\textstyle{X_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{\mathbb{G}A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}}

of
(n+2)
-angles. Then we have a complex

A_{0}\xrightarrow{~\overline{a_{0}}~}A_{1}\xrightarrow{~\overline{a_{1}}~}A_{2}\xrightarrow{~\overline{a_{2}}~}\cdots\xrightarrow{~\overline{a_{n}}~}A_{n+1}\xrightarrow{(-1)^{n}\overline{f_{n+1}}}\mathbb{G}A_{0}.

We define
right
(n+2)
-angles
in
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}
as the complexes which are isomorphic to
complexes obtained in this way. We denote by
\Theta
the class of right
(n+2)
-angles.

The following result was proved in
[
L1
, Lemma 3.5]
for general case.
So their proof can be applied for our case without any change.

Lemma 4.14
.

[
L1
, Lemma 3.5]

Let

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{a_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma f_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{1}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n-1}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma B_{0}}

be a commutative diagram of
(n+2)
-angles in
\mathscr{C}
, where
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
are
\mathscr{X}
-monic. Then we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{0}}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{1}}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{2}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{n-1}}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{n}}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{n}}}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n}\overline{\alpha_{n+1}}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{n+1}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{G}A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{G}\overline{f_{0}}}
\textstyle{B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{0}}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{1}}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{2}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{n-1}}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{n}}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n}\overline{\beta_{n+1}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{G}B_{0}}

of right
(n+2)
-angles in
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}
.

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\Sigma,\Phi)
be an
(n+2)
-angulated category and
\mathscr{X}
be a
strongly covariantly finite subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
.
Then the quotient category
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{X}
is a right
(n+2)
-angulated category
in the sense of Definition
3.1
, where the endofunctor
\mathbb{G}
is defined in Definition
4.12
and right
(n+2)
-angles is defined in Definition
4.13
.

Proof.

We only need to prove (RN1)(b
∗
) and (RN4
∗
), as the proofs for the other axioms are the same as the ones provided in
[
L1
, Theorem 3.7]
and can be omitted.

The commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

shows that

0\rightarrow A\xrightarrow{1}A\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow 0

belongs to
\Theta
. Thus (RN1)(b
∗
) holds.
Now we prove that (RN4
∗
) holds. Given the solid part of the diagram

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{0}}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{1}}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{2}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{n}}}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n}\overline{\alpha_{n+1}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{G}A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{1}a_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a_{0}}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{1}}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{2}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b_{n}}}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n}\overline{\beta_{n+1}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{G}A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{G}\overline{a_{0}}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f_{1}}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{c_{1}}}
\textstyle{C_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{c_{2}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{c_{n}}}
\textstyle{C_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n}\overline{\theta_{n+1}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{G}A_{1}}

with commuting squares and with rows in
\Theta
which are induced by the three
(n+2)
-angles in
\mathscr{C}
as follows

A_{0}\xrightarrow{~a_{0}~}A_{1}\xrightarrow{~a_{1}~}A_{2}\xrightarrow{~a_{2}~}\cdots\xrightarrow{~a_{n}~}A_{n+1}\xrightarrow{a_{n+1}}\Sigma A_{0},

A_{0}\xrightarrow{~f_{1}a_{0}~}B_{1}\xrightarrow{~b_{1}~}B_{2}\xrightarrow{~b_{2}~}\cdots\xrightarrow{~b_{n}~}B_{n+1}\xrightarrow{b_{n+1}}\Sigma A_{0},

A_{1}\xrightarrow{~f_{1}~}B_{1}\xrightarrow{~c_{1}~}C_{2}\xrightarrow{~c_{2}~}\cdots\xrightarrow{~c_{n}~}C_{n+1}\xrightarrow{c_{n+1}}\Sigma A_{1}.

where
a_{0}
and
f_{1}
are
\mathscr{X}
-monic, so is
f_{1}a_{0}
. Apply (N4
∗
) to the above three
(n+2)
-angles, there exist morphisms

f_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow B_{i}~~(i=2,3,\cdots,n+1),g_{i}:B_{i}\rightarrow C_{i}~~(i=2,3,\cdots,n+1),h_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow C_{i-1}~~(i=3,4,\cdots,n+1)

such that each square of the following diagram are commutative

We claim that the morphism
\left[\begin{matrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]:A_{2}\rightarrow A_{3}\oplus B_{2}

is
\mathscr{X}
-monic. In fact, for each morphism
s:A_{2}\rightarrow X
, where
X\in\mathscr{X}
, since
f_{1}
is
\mathscr{X}
-monic, there exist a morphism
t:B_{1}\rightarrow X
such that
sa_{1}=tf_{1}
. It follows that
tf_{1}a_{0}=sa_{1}a_{0}=0
. So there exist a morphism
r:B_{2}\rightarrow X
such that
rb_{1}=t
. Thus we have

(s-rf_{2})a_{1}=sa_{1}-rf_{2}a_{1}=tf_{1}-rb_{1}f_{1}=tf_{1}-tf_{1}=0.

Then there exist a morphism
k:A_{3}\rightarrow X
such that
ka_{2}=s-rf_{2}
.
It follows that
s=ka_{2}+rf_{2}=\left[\begin{matrix}k&r\\
\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
.
This shows that

\left[\begin{matrix}a_{2}\\
f_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right]:A_{2}\rightarrow A_{3}\oplus B_{2}

is
\mathscr{X}
-monic. Hence the sequence (
4.6
) induces a right
(n+2)
-angle

\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{\cdots}

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{c_{n-1}}}
\textstyle{C_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{c_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{C_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n}\overline{\theta_{n+1}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{G}A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{G}\overline{a_{1}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}(-1)^{n}\overline{f_{n+1}}&-\overline{b_{n}}&0\\[2.84526pt]
\overline{h_{n+1}}&\overline{g_{n}}&\overline{c_{n-1}}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]\hskip 19.91692pt}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\oplus C_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{g_{n+1}}~&\overline{c_{n}}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{C_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n}\overline{\gamma_{n+1}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{G}A_{2}}

Thus we have
\overline{\gamma_{n+1}}=\mathbb{G}\overline{a_{1}}\circ\overline{\theta_{n+1}}
.
This shows that (RN4
∗
) holds.∎

5
Axiom (RN3) is redundant

In this section, we will prove that (RN3) is redundant, as it can be deduced from other axioms. In fact, it can be deduced by (RN1)(c) and (RN4
∗
).

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\Sigma,\Theta)
be a right
(n+2)
-angulated category.
Then the axiom
(RN3)
is a consequence of the axioms
(RN1)(c)
and
(RN4
∗
)
.

Proof.

This is an adaptation of the proof of
[
AHBT
, Theorem 3.1]
.
Given the solid part of the commutative diagram

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n-1}}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma f_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{1}}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n-1}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n}\hskip 5.69054pt}
\textstyle{B_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma B_{0}}

with rows in
\Theta
. From the above diagram we have
f_{1}a_{0}=b_{0}f_{0}
, we write it as
c_{0}
, i.e.

c_{0}=f_{1}a_{0}=b_{0}f_{0}.

By (RN1)(c), the three morphisms
c_{0},f_{0},f_{1}
can be respectively embedded into three right
(n+2)
-angles

A_{0}\xrightarrow{~c_{0}~}B_{1}\xrightarrow{~c_{1}~}C_{2}\xrightarrow{~c_{2}~}\cdots\xrightarrow{~c_{n-1}~}C_{n}\xrightarrow{~c_{n}~}C_{n+1}\xrightarrow{~c_{n+1}~}\Sigma A_{0},

A_{0}\xrightarrow{~f_{0}~}B_{0}\xrightarrow{~d_{1}~}D_{2}\xrightarrow{~d_{2}~}\cdots\xrightarrow{~d_{n-1}~}D_{n}\xrightarrow{~d_{n}~}D_{n+1}\xrightarrow{~d_{n+1}~}\Sigma A_{0},

A_{1}\xrightarrow{~f_{1}~}B_{1}\xrightarrow{~e_{1}~}E_{2}\xrightarrow{~e_{2}~}\cdots\xrightarrow{~e_{n-1}~}E_{n}\xrightarrow{~e_{n}~}E_{n+1}\xrightarrow{~e_{n+1}~}\Sigma A_{1}.

Consider the following two commutative diagram
